ParlaMint-ES_2021-10-14-CD211014.u130	Madam President, Mr Guztioi. I am beginning my speech by making the point of our vote in favour of the amendment tabled by the Republican Parliamentary Group, since its central approaches are close to or coincide with our own approaches, exceeding very positively the content of the Government's draft. We share the view of the crimes of Francoism as crimes against humanity, as we share the view of the Amnesty Act of 1977 as contrary to existing international human rights law. We welcome the creation of possible truth commissions, since, while it is not specifically proposed to set up such bodies, a favourable option remains. We believe that there are greater concretions regarding the allocation of the law to Euskal Herria and other peoples and nations of the State, and we welcome the State's approach to the assumption of the responsibilities arising from Francoist repression. We could continue to list the progress observed in this amendment of the Republican Parliamentary Group, but we take this opportunity to show our position on the draft submitted by the Government. With regard to the drafts presented above and the 2007 law, we recognize that important steps have been taken to improve on the current proposal. Society in general and the memorial movement for years have claimed points related to truth, justice and reparation, and thanks to their relentless struggle it has been achieved that they have been translated into this law. However, we believe that there are important aspects that have not been collected and that should be in a law of democratic memory to be considered good law. We see a need for an opening of the time period that the law intends to address. The reparation of victims cannot be limited to the proposed period, closing in 1978 the existence of a violation of rights and thus denying subsequent victims their rights, recognition and reparation. If there is a violation of human rights, torture, arbitrary detention, executions, State terrorism or the suppression of fundamental rights, there must be effective redress. We do not share the praises of the transitional period without taking into account that there was no democratic rupture. An act of recognition and reparation should be given to the victims and should be reflected in this law. It is also consistent with the fact that in the Basque Autonomous Community and in the Foral Community of Navarre there have been laws for the recognition of victims of the State on political grounds in which the PSOE has been, laws after 1978. The PSOE until a recent period - in proposals of law presented on December 27, 2017 and July 19, 2019 - has admitted the consideration of the crimes of Francoism as crimes against humanity. We see a need for this consideration in order to meet the demand for trials for political leaders. The Official Secrets Act should be repealed without further delay. It is unacceptable that information is filtered into which possible limits are contemplated on topics such as 23F, GAL, Zabalza or last week's, the conversations between Manglano, director of the intelligence service, and Antonio Asunción, minister of the socialist interior, in which the latter related his predecessor, José Luis Corcuera, with the sending of letters bomb to militants of the abertzale left who caused the death of José Antonio Cardoso, a young postman of twenty-two years. We also propose that a truth commission be set up to investigate, analyse and propose administrative dossiers for the recognition and reparation of these victims. We share what the UN rapporteur, Fabián Salvioli, put forward in his July 2021 report, highlighting the incompatibility of the Amnesty Act with the obligation to defend Spain's human rights. We propose the need to repeal this law, or at least its most relevant article, and the finding of the imprescriptibility of crimes against humanity committed by Francoism. This estimate should at least allow the possibility of investigating, imputing and prosecuting the perpetrators of these violations, which is consistent with the creation of a special prosecutor by conferring powers on him or her. On the other hand, we see a need for the realization of reparation to all victims of human rights violations. Finally, in the area of competition, we demand respect for the laws of memory that are legislated in our territory. The use of the Constitutional Court and the obligation to readapt texts is a reality in Nafarroa and the Basque Autonomous Community, and we are not prepared to allow new remedies. These will be the principles that guide us in the process of amending the joint text, which we believe can improve the current draft of the Government. Eskerrik asko.

ParlaMint-ES_2021-10-14-CD211014.u132	Thank you very much, President. We are discussing the draft law on democratic memory through amendments to the whole with a request for a return by the VOX and Popular parliamentary groups and with an alternative text by the Republican parliamentary group. Ladies and gentlemen, you have already announced that the Basque Parliamentary Group will not support any of them. With regard to that of the Republican Parliamentary Group, we consider that its amendment to the whole with alternative text can be renewed through partial amendments, both in relation to the amendments it seeks to Act No. 52/2007 and in relation to memorial movements in relation to the Second Republic and certain collectives. Nor do we agree that the bill is an unnecessary text - as is pointed out in the other amendments to the whole - since there has been an unequivocal will for reparation in the last forty years and by different public authorities; that it is encouraged by a revanchist spirit, because it does not leave the judgment of the past to history, or that it is an imposture, since the articles dedicated to the search for the mortal remains of victims do not respond to a real concern for the deceased and their descendants, but rather to maintain the patrimony over the huge number of deaths in the counties. Ladies and gentlemen, it is only by this paragraph of the amendment to the whole of VOX that we believe that we should have the rejection of the rest of the House's forces. Ladies and gentlemen, it is true that in the last forty years there have been acts of reparation - mainly material - to the victims of the dictatorship and the civil war. Executed officials are rehabilitated and pensions, aid and other economic compensation have been granted to relatives of the deceased and disappeared, to maimed persons, to those deprived of their liberty for ideological or political reasons and to children of war. The property seized has been partially restored to trade union organizations and political parties, and nationality has also been granted to international brigades and, although not all, to descendants of immigrants for political or persecution reasons. Finally, it has already been said that Law 52/2007 on historical memory has been a milestone for those who suffered persecution and violence for political, ideological or belief reasons, although we also consider the criticisms of its declaratory character to be adjusted, which is evidenced by both memorialist and victim organizations and by international human rights organizations in relation to treaties ratified by the Spanish State. They therefore demand a more comprehensive and comprehensive nature of the legislation to promote moral reparation and personal and family recovery, in the way of memory policies developed in different countries of the Union - in particular, in Germany -; policies that are in line with the values that constitute the common framework of the Treaty on Union - basically, in its Article 2 - and that are also in line with constitutional values such as the defence of democracy - without prejudice to the fact that the 1978 Constitution is not a militant Constitution -, the promotion of justice, the protection of human rights in their exercise and respect for the dignity of the individual and the rights inherent in it. Ladies and gentlemen, for all this it is necessary that we process the bill, in order to preserve and maintain the memory of the victims of the dictatorship and the civil war, to know the truth, to restore justice, to promote reparation and to promote memory from the public authorities in order to avoid the repetition of any form of political violence or totalitarianism. A law on public policies on the memory of victims. A law, as has already been said, on the recognition, reparation and dignification of those who suffered persecution and violation of their human rights, which constituted an affront to human dignity, in many cases through convictions handed down without minimum guarantees and for ideological and political reasons and not because the crimes of which they were accused are proved, as pointed out in the report of the General Council of the Judiciary, thus emerging from unfounded criticism of the planned ex-lege annulment of sanctions and convictions. In short, ladies and gentlemen, it is not only a materially necessary project, but it is also necessary in the form of a collective memory of painful facts, not leaving them exclusively to the treatment of history, as the People's Parliamentary Group claims in its amendment to the whole, since, as the European Parliament has stated in several resolutions -2009, 2019-, there can be no reconciliation without truth and memory, also outlining the importance of historical memory for the future of the Union. And this is because, following the tragedies of the twentieth century in Europe and precisely as a result of them, the victims become the transmitters of the pains and sufferings they endured so that they are taken into account, not only as a restorative inquiry, but also as a collective duty of memory that arises through Auschwitz to avoid repetitions. These phenomena of memory or social construction of memory, although they have a common substrate, are independent of the purely scientific power of history, which, based on cross-relationships, intertwined, controlled and compared facts, reconstructs what could have happened, integrating itself into an explanatory set but without axiological valuations of good or evil. President, I'm finishing up. Perhaps because of the history of the horrors of the twentieth century in Europe, Ernest Renan's explanation in force since the nineteenth century, that a nation is founded on both common things and that all have forgotten many things, is no longer so fully in force because of the unstoppable outbreak of memorialist movements, and even more so because of institutionalized memorialism. Hence the European Parliament's statement that there can no longer be reconciliation in Europe through oblivion, but through truth and memory. Certainly, this is a process that can lead to tears, but which helps in a solid way the generation of ethically and morally better societies. Thank you very much.

[[Applauds]] Ladies and gentlemen, it is time to verbalise it and the place is this House, because this is where the conditions of possibility lie for us to live together in Spain with our guaranteed sexual freedom and not with sexual violence that is exercised without impunity. Because here, ladies and gentlemen, is where the power to guarantee the rights and freedoms of all citizens lies, but it is particularly important now to guarantee the rights of citizens. The change began long ago on the streets with the feminist movement, and this is where law must be made and extended to all citizens as a guaranteed right. This House is the place to make as many rules as necessary for our freedoms and dignity to be law. What may seem natural to them, which has so far been the normal thing, is not. Machist violence is the most evident sign of the inequality that we suffer women and men; it is the condition of possibility of sexism, and therefore, ladies and gentlemen, Spain needs this law, Spain needs the law of integral guarantee of sexual freedom. It is time to recognize that it is women, girls and boys who suffer sexual violence and that so far there have been very few who have wanted to talk about it, name it and, above all, stop it. But that normalized silence has already broken in the streets and is now beginning to break in institutions as well. With this law, we take a more determined step to ensure that women are citizens of their own right. And it is that, ladies and gentlemen, sexual freedom is a central axis of the right of citizenship, which presupposes the possibility of exercising sovereignty over the first territory of exercise, the body itself; nothing more personal and nothing more political than the body. Sexual freedom is also an elementary condition that opens the door to other rights, in addition to the fundamental right to a life without violence. It opens the door to freedom of movement, to the right to choose where to work, even where to live, and also, of course, opens the door to sexual and reproductive rights. We are therefore aware of the importance of this rule, because we know that it is not enough to enunciate sexual freedom for it to exist in the lives of all women. This law is therefore a cultural change in the rights of victims and an end to impunity. Because in order to transform the usual violence into everyday spaces of freedom, the three fundamental changes that this law entails are necessary. The first, a cultural change, a change in the rules of the game of our society; the change of the culture of rape by the culture of freedom, consent and sexual autonomy. Secondly, a change in the institutional look that symbolizes the 'You are not alone' and the 'I do believe you' of a State that commits itself to the victims and guarantees them an adequate response in their search for assistance, protection, justice and reparation. Thirdly, the end of impunity for sexual violence, especially in certain areas, such as the sexual exploitation industry. These three changes are enormous and, in addition to responding to the legitimate and urgent demands of the feminist movement, involve fulfilling our obligations as a country and as a government within the framework of European and international human rights law. The Istanbul Convention obliges us to change the paradigm of putting consent at the centre of relations. This European framework treaty against all forms of violence against women tells us that the public authorities must guarantee women's sexual freedom and that this does not mean requiring them to prove that they were subjected, that they resisted or that there was violence in a sexual relationship, but that it obliges us to pass laws to place women's consent at the centre, as a central and essential step in guaranteeing their freedom and the right to a life free from violence. With this law, our country joins a list that fortunately grows every year, that of countries that also comply with the Istanbul Convention and that amend their legislation to define as an offence against sexual freedom any sexual act without consent. Belgium, Croatia, Germany, Cyprus, Denmark, Greece, Iceland, Ireland, Luxembourg, Malta, Sweden or the United Kingdom are countries which have already legislated in this regard. The group of experts of the Istanbul Convention, the Grevio, in its first report on Spain, asks us to be the following on the list. In that report, the Grevio welcomes the fact that, through this bill, we send the social message that - it says so textually - rape is rape and that any sexual act carried out with another person without his consent is considered sexual violence, in accordance with article 36 of the Istanbul Convention. But this law, as I say, not only represents a paradigm shift in cultural terms, but also represents a historic advance in material terms, as it fills very important gaps in the path of victims and sets in motion normative instruments to combat impunity for these violences. This rule complies with all the measures set out in the State Covenant against Gender Violence in the field of sexual violence, and even improves the framework of the Covenant itself, thanks to the comprehensiveness of the norm. Specifically, the Act implements measures 182, 183, 188, 8, 184, 3, 5, 186, 29, 135, 185 and 107 of the Views of Congress and measures 171, 173, 178,69, 89, 4, 7, 14 and 15 of the Views of the Senate and establishes for the first time in Spain a catalogue of the rights of victims of sexual violence. The law recognizes the right of victims to specialized health care, to economic and housing autonomy, with a system of aids and labour rights and, of course, also to free legal assistance. Another fundamental right enshrined in this law, which is key in the exit processes of victims, is the right to specialized comprehensive assistance, which is developed through two pioneering services in this country. First, the 24-hour crisis centres for victims of sexual violence, permanent interdisciplinary services, the first link in the support chain and specialized care. I say that they are pioneers because the Council of Europe recommends one centre for every two hundred thousand women and, at present, there are two throughout the state. This number will be increased by provinces, thanks to the project of the Ministry of Equal Investment of European funds. With regard to girls and boys, the law implements the Scandinavian and Anglo-Saxon model of children's homes in Spain. This model aims to combat the revictimization that surrounds many cases of sexual violence against girls and boys through the participation of all professionals involved in the path of care and justice in the same place, in a specific place, adapted and adapted to the needs of girls and boys. This means responding to international recommendations related to the specialization, special protection and non-revictimization of children who are victims of sexual violence. I would like to thank Minister Belarus and her ministry for their work in building this proposal together. [[Applauds]] The law also focuses on alleviating another of the deficits with which a victim is on his or her itinerary, such as the lack of judicial specialization. And it does so not only by establishing the specialization of the judicial body and its holders, but also by training in sexual violence throughout the chain of fundamental legal operators to guarantee the right of victims to justice: the Office of the Prosecutor, the Advocate and, of course, forensic doctors and forensic assessment units. And a fundamental right in this new catalogue that also responds to the fulfilment of our international obligations and that the law develops widely is the right of victims of sexual violence to reparation. The measures of the law are an improvement in the effective access of victims to compensation in proportion to the damage caused, to full recovery, including victims with serious consequences, to the right to non-repetition of violence, through programmes for the protection of victims and the rehabilitation of aggressors, and to social recognition, with measures to restore the dignity of victims and to generate a social narrative that delegitimates violence as well as aggressors. Finally, I would like to refer to another important measure aimed especially at ending the impunity of an industry that moves 5 million euros in black a day and whose lucrative activity is the sexual exploitation of women and girls. This law also comes to comply with the recommendations of the United Nations addressed to our country in the fight against sexual exploitation. In particular, Cedaw, in its last report in 2015, urged us to adopt a broad definition of "proxenetism" in order to enable the proper prosecution of those who exploit prostitution. This law therefore amends the offence of non-coercive pimping, which is currently virtually inapplicable, in the words of the Attorney General's Office, and recovers the figure of the rental third party so that it can effectively prosecute those who use their premises or floors to profit from the sexual exploitation of women. In the amendment of these articles we focus on companies that have sexual exploitation as a regular business, to which measures such as closure of the premises, fine and dissolution of the legal person will also apply, creating, in addition, with the money confiscated from the pimps, a fund to help women victims. I told them that this is the law of cultural change, of the change in the most basic rules of our society, of the rights of the victims and of the end of impunity. For women victims of trafficking and sexual exploitation, who suffer enormous human rights violations without the State reaching them effectively, it is imperative that our institutional action should ensure the fundamental rights of women in prostitution contexts and, at the same time, eliminate impunity for the pimp industry. None of these goals can be achieved without giving determined attention to the other. The fight against impunity for pimps cannot imply the loss of rights or greater vulnerability of women, and the guarantee of women's rights goes hand in hand with the end of impunity for the pimp industry. The State must guarantee and offer to women victims of trafficking and sexual exploitation ways of emancipation that are real, dignified and appropriate to their reality. To this end, the law guarantees these women the same route of comprehensive care and reparation as for the victims of any other sexual violence and, in addition, establishes a fund to finance measures to integrate work and promote economic autonomy, in coordination with autonomous communities and local authorities. In addition, together with the Ministry of Labour and Social Economy, we are putting in place a social and labour integration plan for women victims of sexual exploitation and in prostitution contexts. But, ladies and gentlemen, I believe that this law can be even more ambitious in protecting the rights of women victims of trafficking and sexual exploitation. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, I would also like to raise the possibility of us working in a coordinated way in the parliamentary process of this rule. Ending sexual exploitation and trafficking cannot be a long-term task, and for it to be a task of the present we cannot avoid an obvious reality: more than 70 per cent of women victims of these violence are in an irregular administrative situation, as the entities working on the ground, such as Doctors of the World, point out. The amendment of the Aliens Act is an inescapable step so that these women can access the material conditions that guarantee them an opportunity, so that they can access a temporary residence permit, a housing, so that they can open a current account or access a job or a formative itinerary. The amendment of the Aliens Act is essential so that the proposals we are putting in place can reach all women effectively. I can understand that there are those who say that this law is late, that women, girls and boys in this country have waited too long. What we cannot afford, precisely for this reason, is to tell them that they have to keep waiting. That is why I would like to ask every Member and every Member to sit in this House and debate, to put aside for a moment the need to confront and to talk about the rights and needs of women in this country. On these extremely important observations ignored by his ministry, we have built an amendment to the whole, which focuses on the two major objections that have been transferred to the bill: the definition of express consent, which is necessary for a sexual relationship to be considered, in quotes, consented to, and the disappearance of the distinction in the Criminal Code between abuse and sexual assault. The Government is shielded by an alleged need to modify the current criminal response, referring to Spain's ratification of the 2014 Istanbul Convention of the Council of Europe. Madam Minister, it is not a European framework treaty, it is a Council of Europe convention, but it turns out that there is no reference in the convention to express consent, but it is talking about the voluntary provision of such consent considering the context. I mean, it could be tacitly assumed and deductible. Our legal system has never intended anything different, and it is already based on the idea of consent. How could it be otherwise? The definition that the bill provides of express consent hides an enormous complexity, whose derivations, in the words of one of the authors of the legal text, are the following, and I quote textually: the change of paradigm in the matter of consent is important, because until now the courts could understand that, if you did not pronounce yourself, you were consenting. Now they'll have to do it the other way around, if you don't pronounce yourself, you're denying yourself. That shifts the focus of interest to men. They must ensure that the other person consents, that it is not enough for him to be silent. Where is, Minister, the presumption of innocence in this case? Where is legal certainty? It is an innovative formulation, we have no doubt, which carries undeniable risks, with the possible reversal of the burden of proof and the consequent reduction of the constitutional rights of the person prosecuted. Problems in determining whether there has been a crime against sexual freedom will continue where they were already, in the existence or not of evidence, not in the mere testimony, of which they will continue to need evidence. Nor has it been taken into account that the 2016 European Directive, which reinforces the presumption of innocence, emphasises in its Article 6 that Member States shall ensure that the burden of proof to determine the guilt of suspects and accused persons falls on the prosecution, and that Member States shall ensure that any doubts about guilt always benefit the suspect or accused person. And the second major problem of this text is also reflected in the amendment to the whole: the removal of the distinction between sexual abuse and assault, according to his ministry, also linked to the obligations assumed by Spain with the ratification of the Istanbul Convention. Nothing in the convention requires this amendment; it simply advocates a comprehensive response to sexual violence, just as it obliges women to respond to other types of violence. The elimination of the crime of abuse - as has already been said here - by treating in a uniform manner any attack on sexual freedom, as if all were of the same gravity, entails in practice a reduction of the penalties of the most violent crimes because of the need of the legislator to reduce the punishment originally intended for the most serious cases, in order to respond to those who are less violent. In this way, discretion is allowed in the judicial response that is alarming and that would allow, for example, as some investigators have pointed out, that the members of La Manada of 2016 - yes, minister, the members of La Manada -, sentenced today to a term of fifteen years, could only be sentenced with this text in force to ten years. In short, the result is that the lighter the crime, the greater the penalty proposed, and the more serious the punishment is reduced; an absolute misunderstanding, which we do not know whether it is due to unpredictability, ignorance or the will to reaffirm itself as an anti-system alternative, obviating the clear recommendations of the General Council of the Judiciary in this regard. That is why, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, our parliamentary group has tabled this amendment to the whole of which we are defending today. Minister, you do not have to comply with the requirements of the People's Parliamentary Group; it is the governing bodies of the judges who recommend that you, in your first legislative text, stop happening and, together with this House, draw up a sensible bill, which addresses both the very serious problem of sexual violence and the constitutional rights of all Spaniards. ParlaMint-ES_2021-10-14-CD211014.u187	We turn to the point of committee opinions on legislative initiatives. [[Alicante/Alacant]] Mr LópezBas Valero has the floor on behalf of the Citizens' Parliamentary Group.
ParlaMint-ES_2021-10-14-CD211014.u188	Thank you, President. 'And guide the waters in such a way that they water and pray as much as they used to in Moorish times, and that they make all the lands water for the stops by do they used to take their batch in Moorish times and not for another log'. Ladies and gentlemen, these words that I have just read to you perhaps correspond to one of the oldest examples of a political transition, of the circumstance of changing a political system without changing what works, of that going 'from the law to the law through the law' - so to speak - that took place there by 1275. These words are of the privilege granted by Alfonso X to the regents of the Vega Baja del Segura so that they could maintain their traditional irrigation systems in their fields and labor lands, so that an entire irrigation engineering and a social and economic regime based on agriculture would remain in force, following the advance and conquest by Christian troops in the face of Muslim domination on the Iberian Peninsula. In that 1275 the traditional irrigation uses of the inhabitants and inhabitants of Vega del Segura that had existed since centuries ago were recognized, respected and maintained. For this reason, we are facing an institution, which today represents the Privativo de Aguas de Orihuela and the peoples of its framework, which is millennial; an institution that has remained essentially unchanged for all these centuries, despite the social and political changes of what is now Spain; for, as I say, not years, but centuries of history. It is an institution governed by its own ordinances adopted in 2014 adapting the previous ones of 1836, which in turn replace those of 1625. What we are dealing with here today is to bring to this plenary the opinion of the Committee on Justice after the presentation in charge of the report for the amendment of article 19 of the Organic Law of the Judiciary, in order to introduce into it a fifth paragraph in order to recognize the Privativo de Aguas de Orihuela as a customary and traditional court, as were already the Tribunal de les Aigües de Valencia and the Council of Men Buenos De Murcia. With the proposal to amend this article, the jurisdictional nature of an institution that already has legal recognition in the administrative field as a community of users or a community of regents under article 81 of the current Waters Act is fully and legally recognized; a community that also has the nature of a corporation governed by public law, as recognized in article 82 of that legal text. This public administrative nature allows the Privativo de Aguas to represent and manage the distribution and use of traditional irrigation water, the water coming from the Segura River, between the inheritances or portions of agricultural land irrigated with those waters, traditionally measured in tahulls, octaves, arms and palms. They are lands that are watered through millennial aqueducts called mother or elder acechias, ravines or minor acechias, bracelets and hijoles; lands that eventually drain their waterings into slippers, miserables and miserables, words all of them that in the Alicante garden of Segura sound old and worked hard, words that smell land and water. This traditional irrigation system has led in parallel to the management of water, in relation to the places of access to it and the times of irrigation, known as stops and tandas, respectively, the function of decisions on conflicts between regents, a jurisdictional function that has been given for centuries through the institution and its highest representative, today the judge on demand, president of the Court of Waters of the court, office that today holds by democratic election don Pedro Mompeán Madrid. This is precisely what the present proposed legal amendment deals with, the legal recognition of this jurisdictional nature of the Private Water Court of Orihuela in matters within its scope and according to its competence. It constitutes the consideration of this institution as a means of participation of citizens in the Administration of Justice, as expressly recognized in article 125 of the Spanish Constitution, together with other institutions such as that of the People's Jury or the People's Legislative Initiative. But it also constitutes in this case the recognition of an institution which is an indissoluble part of a people, of its history, of its heritage maintained for centuries, of a way of life and of social and economic activity such as agriculture and, therefore, of the management of that good which is of everyone, which is water. It has been shown that the regents of the Vega Baja del Segura know this good perfectly, that they know how to manage with criteria of efficiency and sustainability, as they have done for centuries and generations, to ensure precisely the continuity of their way of life. This way of life generates, as always, wealth for all of Spain and asks, ladies and gentlemen, memory and respect, calls for opportunities for development in order to be able to continue offering new glories to Spain, as indicated by the anthem of my community, of the Valencian Community; and it does so from that distant south sometimes forgotten of it, from Orihuela and from the Vega Baja del Segura. Ladies and gentlemen, as an Oriolan I am, as a councillor who I have been from my city - so I have also tried and discussed, when necessary, with the Private Water Court of Orihuela - I speak to you in this rostrum from that city, I speak to you from the heart, to inform you, of course, of the favourable vote of my group, Citizens, on this initiative; to request also that of all groups in the same direction, and, finally, to invite you, as it cannot otherwise be, to know when my land and its institutions, among them, and in any case, the Private Water Court of Orihuela and the peoples of its framework, when they so desire. An institution that is of my city, of Orihuela, but also of peoples that I do not want to mention today: Redovan, Bigastro, Jacarilla, Almoradí, Benejuzar, Algorfa, Callosa de Segura and Rafal. Thank you very much. [[The vote will take place tomorrow at 5 p.m.]]

ParlaMint-ES_2021-10-14-CD211014.u194	With your permission, President. Afternoon - afternoon already - Members. 'Near the water I want to take you 'because your curl transcends the sea. / Close to the water I want you to have / because you encourage your vivid being. / Close to the water I want you to feel / because foam teaches you to laugh'. Verses by Miguel Hernández, our poet from Orihuela. Water Verses, an object over which the Private Water Court of Orihuela has jurisdiction. Today we discussed the opinion of the Justice Commission on the proposed organic law amending the Organic Law of the Judiciary, in order to recognize the character of customary and traditional court to the Private Court of Waters of Orihuela and peoples of its framework. The Private Water Court of Orihuela is an entity that has been providing justice and managing irrigation for over a thousand years in the region. The irrigation in the Vega Baja del Segura began in isolation in Roman times, but it is during the Arab domination when the irrigation is implemented, an infrastructure is created and the administration and delivery of justice is created. [[Rumors]] Since this time, the Private Water Court of Orihuela has been administering irrigation and providing justice on more than 7,500 hectares of its jurisdiction. The Privativo de Aguas Court has been governed and presided over, as has been said, by a board of government formed by the water judge, who presides, the lieutenant judge and the syndicate general, all of whom are elected in Orihuela, your people and mine, the historic capital of the province of Alicante. Without this capital it would not be understood the existence of a water court, as it has historical capitals located in irrigation areas, such as Murcia and Valencia. [[Rumors]] The Vega Baja alicantina, together with the Murcia Garden and the irrigation areas of Almeria form an economic pole linked to the primary sector that is essential for the economy of southeastern Spain and that is the pantry of Europe. Every drop of water used in this area multiplies jobs and wealth and no one knows better than the farmers of the southeastern Spain how to adjust consumption so as not to waste a drop of water. For this reason, it is not understood why socialists and communists, whenever they have occasion, punish us the Alicantens, the Murcians and the Almerians by drowning our source of life more and more deeply: the Tajo Segura transfer. [[Applauds]] The transfer is the result of a policy of spatial planning, a successful policy that has allowed the consolidation of an agricultural sector of enormous vitality in the south-east of Spain and, in turn, has ensured the water supply of the urban supplies of numerous populations of the province, including large capitals such as Alicante, Elche, Torrevieja and Orihuela. Around 60% of the water for human consumption in the area comes from the transfer, making it a maintainer of the supply system. The surface watered with the flows of the Tajo Segura transfer covers more than 60% of the provincial irrigation, turning the orchards into real CO2 sinks. The drip irrigation is installed in more than 90% of that cultivated space; the gross economic value of the Tajo Segura aqueduct areas in the province of Alicante is more than EUR 500 million per year; the energy cost of transvase and postrasvase is 1.1 kilowatts per cubic meter, making its waters irreplaceable for irrigation, as the alternative of desalinated water is four times more expensive and environmentally harmful. But the transfers begin to be for the current government miracles of hydraulic engineering of the seventies of the 20th century in the process of extinction. That is, that the Executive, already without caps, returns to the Narbona clause, and its objective, allied with the radicalism of climate change, is the closure of an infrastructure without which it would be impossible to explain the development of the Vega Baja in the last forty years. Today the transfer is wounded to death. The Government condemns us to supply desalinated water, a resource that, in addition to the current finding that the boron containing the fields is drying, requires an energy consumption of more than 4 kilowatts per cubic metre and produces a large amount of waste in the desalinators, especially the brine, which is very toxic because it is composed of chemicals used in the desalination process, such as copper and chlorine. Vice President Ribera could visit Torrevieja and check how the macroplant driven by her partner Narbona, which cost more than 350 million euros, is not even connected to supply water to the 30,000 farmers scheduled. ParlaMint-ES_2021-10-14-CD211014.u198	Thank you very much, Madam President. 'So much river that goes to the sea/where no water is needed. / So many fields that dry out. Ladies and gentlemen, these verses are from our Oriolan and universal poet Miguel Hernández, and precisely to claim and regulate the proper use of water in the Orihuela orchard has been occupied since time immemorial, as has been said, by the Privativo de Aguas de Orihuela. Be my first words of appreciation to the President of the Water Court of the Private Water Court of Orihuela and to all the trustees and presidents who throughout history with good governance have managed the uses of water in the Vega Baja of the province of Alicante. It originated, as has also been said, in the privilege of 14 May 1275 of King Alfonso X the Wise. This is the first written standard known as water regulator in the Vega Baja del Segura. The news that is kept about these risks only reaches at the time of the conquest of Orihuela in the disposition of King Alfonso X the Wise, who proceeded to the distribution of the lands of the Vega Baja, although some attribute this great work to the Romans and others attribute it to the times of the Arabs. For more than 350 years, the regulations contained in the privilege constitute the management of the irrigation of the Oriolan garden. Jaime II of Aragon confirmed in the year 1323 the previous royal privileges and emphasized that the water affairs were exclusive competence of the envelope, not even allowing that the governor interfered in his decisions. Ladies and gentlemen, making a little more history, on 16 June 1501 Fernando the Catholic declared the same provisions and that the appeals were known to the jurors and council of the city; he even fined 1000 guilders of gold to the authorities who intervened in these matters of irrigation. These laws and ordinances remained in force, always responding with good government to the repeated complaints raised to the sovereign about abuses in the regime and distribution of water, such as the neglect of the cleaning of sieges and mischiefs, which had serious damage to agriculture, to real interests and also to particular interests. They are some examples of a court that throughout history has left a mark on the good work done in water management and its uses. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, from a long time ago in history, together with the Court of the Waters of Valencia, and the Council of Men Buenos de Murcia, the Private Court of Waters of Orihuela has been governing the territories of the Segura River, exercising administrative and jurisdictional authority and resolving disputes between regents, heirs and others concerning the issue of irrigation and its waters. The court, with the passage of time and divided the ancient jurisdiction of Orihuela, dismembered, forming new ones in Almoradi, Callosa de Segura and Guardamar, replicas of the former. Today's regulations have been adapted to the different water laws - from 1866, 1879 and 1985 -, to the Spanish Constitution and also to the Statute of Autonomy of the Valencian Community. Ladies and gentlemen, the Private Water Court of Orihuela continues today, in the year 2021, managing the irrigation of the Vega Baja del Segura region and complying with the rules established centuries ago. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, we are facing a respected and protected historical jewel. The Private Water Court of Orihuela is an entity that not only administers justice because, in addition, among the regents, it is an organ of peace. We owe a debt to Orihuela, Vega Baja, the province of Alicante and the Valencian Community. The recognition of the Private Water Court of Orihuela as a customary and traditional court and its inclusion as such in the Organic Law of the Judiciary is a right that has thousands of farmers who have historically been managing the proper use of water in a sustainable and responsible manner. But from the People's Parliamentary Group we do not want to stay here and with the history that supports us we urge that, after this approval, the process be started so that it is also declared an Intangible Heritage of Humanity by UNESCO, as are already the Court of Waters of Valencia and the Council of Men Buenos de Murcia. The farmers of Vega Baja, the neighbors of Orihuela, the history that precedes them and the future generations of Oriolans and Alicante deserve this recognition. I would also like to mention the enormous value of the Vega Baja garden of the Segura River for the cultural, social, economic and environmental landscape of the Mediterranean arc and the extraordinary role that has played throughout history in the good management of water in this wonderful land. In these times, in which we debate so much about the uses of water and its management, it is essential that, through the recognition of what we are witnessing today, we highlight what has been a commitment of the neighbors of Vega Baja and Alicante over the centuries to the uses of water, to the protection of the territory, to environmental sustainability and to making it possible for the garden of Europe - as you know the Vega Baja - to be a place with enormous environmental wealth and, at the same time, compatible with being a source of opportunities for its people. To recognize the character of a customary and traditional court at the Privativo de Aguas, therefore, is an act of justice and a further reason for pride for all Alicantens. I would not like to end my speech without sending a message to the Members of the Socialist Party and the political groups supporting the Government. Today we agree, we come to a consensus to put in value and recognize the good management of the millennial water of the people of Orihuela. From now on, let us also agree that the Vega Baja, Alicante, the southeast of Spain and the Levante never lack water and we can continue in the future recognizing and highlighting the good use of the water of that land. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

The first is that of the existence of a reserve of activity for the exercise of the power of attorney, allowing lawyers also to exercise as prosecutors. A ban on multidisciplinary societies that may include advocacy and solicitorship. And the modification of the tariff system. Indeed, the Senate's amendments are further improving, if any, the text that came out of the Congressional Justice Commission, working from dialogue and consensus with all political groups. Ladies and gentlemen, with this project we have made the lawyers and the procurator's office at the European level, giving the candidates flexibility in their ability to direct their career towards one or the other professional exercise. Amendment 3 is incorporated so that professional societies that so far combine a single profession can include the figure of the prosecutor and protect independence and respect for matters being dealt with within the same professional society, including as a new modification the forecasts of the ethical standards of the respective professions. It also includes the possibility of guardianship in external practices when it can be carried out by a procurator and is requested by those who will carry out the practices. Those officials of Group A who have been granted a degree in law and who perform legal assistance or legal advice functions shall be exempted from obtaining the professional qualification prescribed by this law. We have worked in inclusive language - and I say this in the session that we have experienced so important today - and where the law of lawyers and prosecutors was said today there is talk of the law of law and the procuratorship. We have heard a lot about the elimination of tariffs to the prosecutor's office, but what has not been explained is that it was an imposition of the European Services Directive, which imposed on Spain its elimination, but still managed to maintain it in exchange for eliminating minimum tariffs and limiting ceilings. These are measures which, above all, seek consumer protection, which in these cases is usually the weakest link. The establishment of the maximum overall amount of EUR 75 000 for rights earned by procurators in the same procedure has been another element of debate and constant criticism by the People's Party and VOX. But what has not been said is that the law itself incorporates that, exceptionally and justifiedly, this limit may be exceeded provided that the judge authorizes it. In addition, the extension of the deadline for adoption is added as an amendment. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to end by thanking the political groups that have constructively improved this text. Today we are starting a path towards the homogenization of these professions. Above all, I would like to thank these professionals for their efforts over the last few months of the pandemic.
ParlaMint-ES_2021-10-14-CD211014.u216	Mrs. Araujo, you have to finish, please.
ParlaMint-ES_2021-10-14-CD211014.u217	And above all, I want to highlight their constant adaptation. Thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen. [[Applauds]]
